```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\\n\\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\\nThis explains why \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\\n\\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information actively \\\\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\\\% (Table~\\\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\\\%.\\n\\nThe component ablation (Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}) reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) each achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% individually, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA does not improve over the best Tier~1 signal, suggesting a practical design principle: invest extraction effort in preserving high-specificity diagnostic events and discard lower-tier runtime details.\\nFurther analyses of training dynamics, error patterns, and context length sensitivity are reported in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_dynamics_appendix}--\\\\ref{sec:context_length_appendix}.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Limitations.}\\nOur study has several limitations.\\nFirst, all experiments use a single benchmark suite (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); although DebugBench cross-dataset validation confirms the component hierarchy (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:debugbench}), generalization to other programming languages---whose exception semantics differ from Python's (e.g., Go's panic/recover, Rust's Result types)---remains untested.\\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the ${\\\\sim}$7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\\nThird, DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% ($\\\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% for the outlier vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for others); Qwen shows zero variance across seeds.\\nFourth, all experiments use LoRA fine-tuning; whether full fine-tuning would eliminate seed sensitivity or alter the relative ranking of trace conditions is unknown.\\nFifth, training and test data originate from the same benchmark suite (different problems), potentially inflating accuracy estimates relative to out-of-distribution deployment.\\nFinally, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one evaluation model; while this does not affect verdict prediction (classifying a \\\\emph{given} solution), the resulting class balance depends on the generator's coding ability.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Future Work.}\\nSeveral directions merit investigation: (1)~extending the component hierarchy analysis to additional languages (Java, C++, Rust) where exception and error-handling semantics differ substantially; (2)~scaling to larger models (13B, 70B) to test whether the Tier~1/Tier~2 boundary shifts with model capacity; (3)~evaluating on more diverse benchmarks including LiveCodeBench and real-world CI/CD failure logs; and (4)~deploying exception-based verdict prediction in production test pipelines to measure practical throughput and latency gains over full-trace approaches.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.43}}"}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Method}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Task Definition}\\n\\nWe study \\\\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\\nThe model produces a single token---\\\\texttt{pass} or \\\\texttt{fail}---making this a binary classification task.\\nUnlike code generation \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates fault detection from execution simulation.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Dataset}\\n\\\\label{sec:dataset}\\n\\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each with \\\\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\\\% pass, 33\\\\% fail).\\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples; majority-class baseline is 71.51\\\\%.\\nThe \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\\\% train duplication, 48.99\\\\% test), since correct executions produce no exception events regardless of solution logic.\\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Trace Representations}\\n\\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\\n\\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (source code only),\\n\\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete line-by-line trace),\\n\\\\texttt{VOTA} (exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression),\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (only exception/error events),\\n\\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (only assertion-point actual/expected pairs),\\n\\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only} (only loop compression summaries),\\nand \\\\texttt{VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae} (VOTA without assertion-point information).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\\n\\\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (75.13\\\\%), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (81.76\\\\%), VOTA (99.41\\\\%), and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%). Accuracies: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$.}\\n\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Models}\\n\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B scale:\\n\\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{qwen2025coder} (5.5T pretraining tokens, primary model for all ablations) and\\n\\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{deepseek2024coder} (2T tokens, cross-model validation).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\n\\nWe fine-tune with LoRA~\\\\citep{hu2022lora} applied to all linear layers (rank=16, $\\\\alpha$=32); full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_details}.\\nAll conditions use identical configurations: 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, learning rate $2 \\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\\nInput sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the verdict label.\\nThe primary seed is 42; robustness seeds are 123 and 456.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\n\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace\\\\_label\\\\_only} variant that uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token.\\nIf this improves over standard \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\emph{information overload}.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.51}}"}
```

```text
Interesting. The training actually completed successfully — epoch 3/3, saved checkpoint, eval ran, accuracy = 99.1%. But the system notification says "failed". 

Key finding: ae_only_seed123 accuracy = 99.1%, which is LOWER than seed42's 99.92%. The difference is 0.82pp. This is notable — ae_only was one of the highest conditions (99.92% for seed42). Let me look more carefully:

- Running accuracy drops at [500/1190] from 0.9978 to 0.9780. That's a sudden drop of ~2pp, suggesting a batch of errors starting around sample 500.


- Final accuracy: 99.1% with CI [98.5%, 99.6%]
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Key findings:

1. **ae_only_seed123**: Completed successfully! Accuracy = 99.1% (vs seed42's 99.92%). This is a 0.82pp drop. The "failed" cron might have been triggered by the Agent's post-processing failing or some other issue, not by the training itself.

2. **ae_only_seed456**: Still RUNNING in registry but the log shows training completed (444/444 steps, model saved). It's now in the evaluation phase - loading the checkpoint. The registry hasn't been updated yet.


3. **Registry oddity**: ae_only_seed123 doesn't appear in the registry at all, which suggests the Agent failed during post-processing rather than during training itself. The 99.1% result is notably lower than seed42's 99.92% (a 0.82pp gap), which is interesting since exception_only showed zero variance across all three seeds at 99.83%.

Checking the confidence interval for ae_only: the cluster CI is [99.75, 100.00], and 99.1% falls below that lower bound, meaning seed123 is statistically distinct from seed42. Looking at the per-class metrics more closely, the pass recall dropped to 0.99 and fail precision to 0.97 compared to seed42's perfect 1.00 scores, accounting for roughly 11 total errors across the 1190 samples.

This creates a problem for the narrative—ae_only was positioned as the top performer at 99.92%, but if seed123 is at 99.1%, the mean across seeds could still land in Tier 1 depending on seed456, though the variance is higher than anticipated. I'm also noticing the cron reported failure but the experiment actually completed; the issue might be in the Agent's result registration rather than the experiment itself. The accuracy trajectory is also worth flagging: it held at 100% through sample 350, then degraded to 99.71% at that point, and further to 97.80% by sample 500.

Looking at the math, those 11 errors across 1190 total samples are concentrated in the 350-500 range, which aligns with what the cluster-robust analysis showed—errors aren't random but concentrated in specific problems. In seed42 those problematic cases happened to be classified correctly, but seed123 got them wrong. I should report both the variance concern and the error clustering pattern.
```

_source line 503 · 2026-05-16T06:17:59.079Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all three seeds for ae_only:

- seed42: 99.92% (from registry)
- seed123: 99.08% (from eval_results.json)
- seed456: 99.92% (from eval_results.json — 0.9991596638655462 ≈ 99.92%)

So:
- seed42 = 99.92%
- seed123 = 99.08% — outlier!
- seed456 = 99.92%

Mean = (99.92 + 99.08 + 99.92) / 3 = 298.92 / 3 = 99.64%


Standard deviation comes out to 0.49%, giving me 99.64% ± 0.49% for ae_only. Seed123 stands out as a clear outlier at 99.08% compared to the other two at 99.92%, which reminds me of a similar pattern I saw with DeepSeek.

Looking at exception_only's seed distribution, all three seeds hit 99.83% with zero variance, whereas ae_only shows meaningful variation driven by that problematic seed123. The 0.84 percentage point gap is significant even though 99.08% is still quite strong. For the paper, this means ae_only's reported mean of 99.64% ± 0.49% falls below the single-seed 99.92%, while exception_only remains more stable. The tier hierarchy doesn't shift, but the ranking within Tier 1 does get affected. What's particularly interesting is the error pattern in seed123—11 pass samples were misclassified as fail, which explains the lower recall there.

This error direction is actually the opposite of what format marker artifacts would produce, since those would typically cause false positives on marker-bearing samples. The cron notification failure turned out to be a red herring; the training itself completed successfully, and the issue was just in the post-processing step.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all three seeds for ae_only:

| Seed | Accuracy | Errors |
|------|----------|--------|
| 42   | 99.92%   | 1      |
| 123  | 99.08%   | 11     |
| 456  | 99.92%   | 1      |

Mean = (99.92 + 99.08 + 99.92) / 3 = 298.92 / 3 = 99.64%


Now I'm calculating the standard deviation, which comes out to 0.40 percentage points. So ae_only has a mean accuracy of 99.64% with relatively low variance, but when I compare this to exception_only at 99.83% mean with zero variance, ae_only is showing more variability across seeds.

Looking at the individual results, seed123 is a clear outlier with 11 errors compared to just 1 error in the other two seeds. The paper originally reported ae_only at 99.92% based only on seed42, but across all three seeds the mean drops to 99.64% with a range from 99.08% to 99.92%. This variance might explain why the cron job flagged it as a failure—the post-processing script was probably expecting ae_only to consistently match seed42's performance.

Even with this variance, ae_only's mean of 99.64% still qualifies as Tier 1, and I need to check whether seed123's 99.08% falls within the confidence bounds of exception_only to assess if this actually changes the paper's conclusions.

Actually, looking more carefully: ae_only's worst case at 99.08% falls below exception_only's lower confidence bound of 99.39%, which is problematic. The data now shows exception_only is more reliable with 99.83% ± 0.00% across all seeds, while ae_only has higher variance at 99.64% ± 0.40%. This means ae_only isn't consistently better than exception_only—seed42's advantage disappears when looking at the full picture. This is a significant finding that needs to be communicated to the Director.

There's also a discrepancy with ae_only_seed123: cron marked it as failed, but the experiment actually produced valid results. The failure seems to be in the Agent's post-processing rather than the experiment itself, and notably, ae_only_seed123 isn't even in the registry. I need to flag this inconsistency when reporting to the Director.
```

```text
Now I have the full picture:

1. **ae_only_seed123**: Registry says "failed" with reason "process dead 5× with no sentinel/W&B — auto-resolved as failed". But my independent verification shows the experiment actually COMPLETED SUCCESSFULLY with 99.08% accuracy. The "failed" status is a false positive from the monitoring system (probably couldn't find W&B data or sentinel file, even though the process finished and wrote results).

2. **ae_only_seed456**: Still marked RUNNING in registry, but I saw it completed training + eval. eval_results.json shows 99.92% accuracy. 

3. **New experiments PREPARING**: loop_only_seed123/456, exception_only_nomarker_seed42. These are new since my last check.

The Agent hasn't processed its completion yet. I need to post the critical finding about ae_only_seed123 being falsely marked as failed, then share the ae_only seed robustness analysis showing seed123 at 99.08% (which is lower than seed42's 99.92% and changes the ranking), and review the worker_ssh_check_training_progress notification.

Looking at the ae_only seed results across all three seeds, I'm seeing seed42 and seed456 both at 99.92% with just 1 error each, while seed123 is significantly lower at 99.08% with 11 errors — and it's been incorrectly marked as failed. The mean across these three is 99.64% with a standard deviation of about 0.40 percentage points, which is notably less stable than exception_only's consistent 99.83% across all seeds with zero variance. This is a significant discrepancy from what the paper currently reports using only seed42's 99.92%, so I'll need to flag that the reported accuracy should either be updated to reflect the 3-seed mean or at minimum include the variance data.

I'm also checking the worker notification from yesterday (worker_ssh_check_training_progress_20260515T140932) to see what needs attention there.
```

only.}\\n\\\\label{tab:cross_model}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nUnder \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Qwen achieves 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 across three seeds, while DeepSeek achieves 99.33\\\\% on 2/3 seeds (seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% due to LoRA initialization sensitivity).\\nEven the outlier seed outperforms VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models.\\nUnder VOTA, the gap widens to 14.96pp (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:cross_model_gap}): post-hoc tokenizer analysis reveals that 91.7\\\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples exceed the 2{,}048-token context window due to longer tokenization, causing training on truncated diagnostic information (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\\nA group analysis confirms: intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\\\% while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\\\% (Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$).\\nIn contrast, \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} is unaffected because exception-based inputs fit within the context window for both tokenizers (median 314 tokens).\\nWithout trace information, both models hover near the majority baseline (75.13\\\\%/73.36\\\\%).\\nQwen \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} shows zero seed variance ($\\\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$), while DeepSeek exhibits bimodal behavior consistent with LoRA sensitivity on the smaller model (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:robustness_analysis}).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Component Ablation}\\n\\\\label{sec:ablation}\\n\\nWe decompose the VOTA pipeline into individual components on Qwen-2.5-7B (Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\small\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\\n\\\\toprule\\n\\\\textbf{Condition} & \\\\textbf{Components} & \\\\textbf{Acc (\\\\%)} & \\\\textbf{95\\\\% CI} & \\\\textbf{Tier} \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\midrule\\nae\\\\_only & assertion-point & \\\\textbf{99.92} & [99.75, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\\\\\\nexception\\\\_only & exception events & 99.83 & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.41 & [98.82, 99.83] & --- \\\\\\\\\\nVOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\\\\\\\\\nloop\\\\_only & loop compression & 85.63 & [83.53, 87.56] & 2 \\\\\\\\\\nfull\\\\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\\\\\\\\\nno\\\\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\\\\\\\\\n\\\\bottomrule\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\n\\\\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Tier~1 signals individually achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance.}\\n\\\\label{tab:ablation}\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\nTier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (99.92\\\\%) and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%) are not distinguishable ($p{=}0.80$), and both exceed VOTA (99.41\\\\%, $p{=}0.01$).\\nCombining signals introduces noise rather than complementary information.\\nRemoving assertion points from VOTA has no effect (VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae = VOTA = 99.41\\\\%), confirming exception events dominate within the mixture.\\nTier~2 signal \\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only} (85.63\\\\%) improves over \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} by 10.50pp ($p{<}0.0001$) but remains far below exception events.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}\\n\\\\label{sec:density}\\n\\nThe 17.65pp gap between \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (81.76\\\\%) and VOTA (99.41\\\\%) decomposes into two mechanisms.\\nA label-only loss variant isolating gradient dilution improves full-trace accuracy from 81.76\\\\% to 86.22\\\\% (+4.46pp), but remains 13.19pp below VOTA (99.41\\\\%), indicating that gradient dilution accounts for only 26\\\\% of the gap while information overload accounts for 74\\\\% (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:density_analysis}).\\n\\nTrace length ablation (256--$\\\\infty$ tokens) confirms that raw trace length does not explain the performance gap; all full-trace variants remain within a 5pp range and at least 18pp below \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\\n\\nCalibration analysis shows that Tier~1 conditions achieve near-perfect calibration (ECE$<$0.003), while \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} exhibit substantial miscalibration (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:calibration_full}).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\\n\\\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\\n\\\\end{figure}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation: DebugBench}\\n\\\\label{sec:debugbench}\\n\\nTo test generalization, we evaluate on DebugBench \\\\citep{tian2024debugbench} (426 buggy Python snippets spanning logic, reference, and syntax bugs).\\nThe hierarchy is confirmed: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} achieves 100\\\\% (426/426; CI [99.14, 100.00]), VOTA 99.77\\\\% (425/426), and \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} 96.71\\\\% (CI [94.80, 98.40]).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Regex Baseline Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:regex_baseline}\\n\\nWe evaluate five regex baselines that detect error patterns in traces: B1 (``ERROR'' presence, 93.45\\\\%), B2 (``Traceback'', 71.51\\\\%), B3 (any error keyword, 93.45\\\\%), B4 (exception type extraction, 93.61\\\\%), and B5 (format marker, 99.33\\\\%).\\n\\nThe strongest \\\\emph{legitimate} regex baseline (B4) achieves 93.61\\\\%, 6.22pp below SFT \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%; McNemar $p{<}0.001$).\\nThe SFT model correctly classifies 74 samples that B4 misses, while B4 captures only 2 that SFT misses.\\n\\nB5 exploits the \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker---a preprocessing artifact inserted by our pipeline for passing tests that produce no exception output---achieving 99.33\\\\%.\\nThis marker is not a genuine exception semantic signal but a data preprocessing artifact that leaks the verdict through surface formatting.\\nThe B5--SFT gap is only 0.50pp (McNemar $p{=}0.041$), but through fundamentally different mechanisms: B5 relies on a single format string while SFT interprets exception context (type, message, location) jointly to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions.\\nThis confirms the SFT model captures exception semantics beyond pattern matching (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.47}}"}

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Conclusion}\\n\\nWe presented the first systematic study of what execution trace components drive verdict prediction performance.\\nThrough component ablation across two code LLMs, we established three findings:\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(1) Exception signal transfer.}\\nException events alone achieve 99.83\\\\% $\\\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity.\\nEven the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models; training stability is a secondary factor for smaller models.\\nIn contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(2) Information density decomposition.}\\nThe 17.65pp gap between full traces and VOTA decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\\\%, from training on irrelevant trace tokens) and information overload (74\\\\%, from processing irrelevant trace lines at inference).\\nThe primary bottleneck is trace content selection, not loss design.\\n\\n\\\\textbf{(3) Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy.}\\nTier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA slightly degrades performance (99.41\\\\%), confirming that additional trace components introduce noise rather than complementary signal.\\n\\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to additional datasets (e.g., LiveCodeBench), larger model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.5}}"}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Discussion}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\\n\\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\\nThis explains why \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\\n\\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information actively \\\\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\\\% (Table~\\\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\\\%.\\n\\nThe component ablation (Table~\\\\ref{tab:ablation}) reveals a two-tier diagnostic hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) each achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.8\\\\% individually, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\\\\%.\\nCombining tiers in VOTA does not improve over the best Tier~1 signal, suggesting a practical design principle: invest extraction effort in preserving high-specificity diagnostic events and discard lower-tier runtime details.\\nFurther analyses of training dynamics, error patterns, and context length sensitivity are reported in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_dynamics_appendix}--\\\\ref{sec:context_length_appendix}.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Limitations.}\\nOur study has several limitations.\\nFirst, all experiments use a single benchmark suite (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); although DebugBench cross-dataset validation confirms the component hierarchy (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:debugbench}), generalization to other programming languages---whose exception semantics differ from Python's (e.g., Go's panic/recover, Rust's Result types)---remains untested.\\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the ${\\\\sim}$7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\\nThird, DeepSeek \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\\\% ($\\\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\\\% for the outlier vs.\\\\ 100\\\\% for others); Qwen shows zero variance across seeds.\\nFourth, all experiments use LoRA fine-tuning; whether full fine-tuning would eliminate seed sensitivity or alter the relative ranking of trace conditions is unknown.\\nFifth, training and test data originate from the same benchmark suite (different problems), potentially inflating accuracy estimates relative to out-of-distribution deployment.\\nFinally, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one evaluation model; while this does not affect verdict prediction (classifying a \\\\emph{given} solution), the resulting class balance depends on the generator's coding ability.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Future Work.}\\nSeveral directions merit investigation: (1)~extending the component hierarchy analysis to additional languages (Java, C++, Rust) where exception and error-handling semantics differ substantially; (2)~scaling to larger models (13B, 70B) to test whether the Tier~1/Tier~2 boundary shifts with model capacity; (3)~evaluating on more diverse benchmarks including LiveCodeBench and real-world CI/CD failure logs; and (4)~deploying exception-based verdict prediction in production test pipelines to measure practical throughput and latency gains over full-trace approaches.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Method}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Task Definition}\\n\\nWe study \\\\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\\nThe model produces a single token---\\\\texttt{pass} or \\\\texttt{fail}---making this a binary classification task.\\nUnlike code generation \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates fault detection from execution simulation.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Dataset}\\n\\\\label{sec:dataset}\\n\\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each with \\\\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\\\% pass, 33\\\\% fail).\\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples; majority-class baseline is 71.51\\\\%.\\nThe \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\\\% train duplication, 48.99\\\\% test), since correct executions produce no exception events regardless of solution logic.\\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Trace Representations}\\n\\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\\n\\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (source code only),\\n\\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete line-by-line trace),\\n\\\\texttt{VOTA} (exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression),\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (only exception/error events),\\n\\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (only assertion-point actual/expected pairs),\\n\\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only} (only loop compression summaries),\\nand \\\\texttt{VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae} (VOTA without assertion-point information).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\\n\\\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (75.13\\\\%), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (81.76\\\\%), VOTA (99.41\\\\%), and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%). Accuracies: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$.}\\n\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Models}\\n\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B scale:\\n\\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{qwen2025coder} (5.5T pretraining tokens, primary model for all ablations) and\\n\\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{deepseek2024coder} (2T tokens, cross-model validation).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\n\\nWe fine-tune with LoRA~\\\\citep{hu2022lora} applied to all linear layers (rank=16, $\\\\alpha$=32); full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_details}.\\nAll conditions use identical configurations: 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, learning rate $2 \\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\\nInput sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the verdict label.\\nThe primary seed is 42; robustness seeds are 123 and 456.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\n\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace\\\\_label\\\\_only} variant that uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token.\\nIf this improves over standard \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\emph{information overload}.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.49}}"}
```

Accuracies: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$.}\\\\n\\\\\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\\\n\\\\\\\\end{figure*}\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection{Models}\\\\n\\\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B scale:\\\\n\\\\\\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\\\\\citep{qwen2025coder} (5.5T pretraining tokens, primary model for all ablations) and\\\\n\\\\\\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\\\\\citep{deepseek2024coder} (2T tokens, cross-model validation).\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\\\n\\\\nWe fine-tune with LoRA~\\\\\\\\citep{hu2022lora} applied to all linear layers (rank=16, $\\\\\\\\alpha$=32); full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\\\\\\\ref{sec:training_details}.\\\\nAll conditions use identical configurations: 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, learning rate $2 \\\\\\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\\\\nInput sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the verdict label.\\\\nThe primary seed is 42; robustness seeds are 123 and 456.\\\\n\\\\n\\\\\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\\\n\\\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\\\\\texttt{full\\\\\\\\_trace\\\\\\\\_label\\\\\\\\_only} variant that uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token.\\\\nIf this improves over standard \\\\\\\\texttt{full\\\\\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\\\\\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\\\\\emph{information overload}.\\\\n\\\", \\\"stderr\\\": \\\"\\\", \\\"return_code\\\": 0}, \\\"error\\\": null, \\\"metadata\\\": {\\\"elapsed_seconds\\\": 0.49}}\"}",

```json
      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Method}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Task Definition}\\n\\nWe study \\\\emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.\\nThe model produces a single token---\\\\texttt{pass} or \\\\texttt{fail}---making this a binary classification task.\\nUnlike code generation \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates fault detection from execution simulation.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Dataset}\\n\\\\label{sec:dataset}\\n\\nWe sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\\\\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \\\\citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \\\\citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each with \\\\texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\\\\% pass, 33\\\\% fail).\\nThe dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples; majority-class baseline is 71.51\\\\%.\\nThe \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\\\\% train duplication, 48.99\\\\% test), since correct executions produce no exception events regardless of solution logic.\\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Trace Representations}\\n\\nWe define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:trace_overview}):\\n\\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (source code only),\\n\\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (complete line-by-line trace),\\n\\\\texttt{VOTA} (exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression),\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (only exception/error events),\\n\\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} (only assertion-point actual/expected pairs),\\n\\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only} (only loop compression summaries),\\nand \\\\texttt{VOTA\\\\_no\\\\_ae} (VOTA without assertion-point information).\\n\\n\\\\begin{figure*}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\includegraphics[width=\\\\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}\\n\\\\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Four conditions vary the trace information: \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} (75.13\\\\%), \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (81.76\\\\%), VOTA (99.41\\\\%), and \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (99.83\\\\%). Accuracies: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$.}\\n\\\\label{fig:trace_overview}\\n\\\\end{figure*}\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Models}\\n\\nWe evaluate two instruction-tuned code LLMs at the 7B scale:\\n\\\\textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{qwen2025coder} (5.5T pretraining tokens, primary model for all ablations) and\\n\\\\textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \\\\citep{deepseek2024coder} (2T tokens, cross-model validation).\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Training Setup}\\n\\nWe fine-tune with LoRA~\\\\citep{hu2022lora} applied to all linear layers (rank=16, $\\\\alpha$=32); full hyperparameters in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:training_details}.\\nAll conditions use identical configurations: 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, learning rate $2 \\\\times 10^{-4}$ with linear decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.\\nInput sequences exceeding this limit are truncated from the input content while preserving the verdict label.\\nThe primary seed is 42; robustness seeds are 123 and 456.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Loss Ablation}\\n\\nTo disentangle training signal quality from input signal quality, we introduce a \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace\\\\_label\\\\_only} variant that uses the same full trace as input but computes loss only on the verdict token.\\nIf this improves over standard \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace}, the improvement is attributable to \\\\emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA is then attributable to \\\\emph{information overload}.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.49}}"
```

```text
{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Related Work}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}\\nTracePile \\\\citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \\\\citet{wang2025dcsh} found that trace utility is task- and format-dependent across synthesis, repair, and reasoning; NExT \\\\citep{ni2024next} used trace chain-of-thought for program repair; ET-CoT \\\\citep{eguchi2025etcot} introduced sub-line granularity traces.\\n\\\\citet{jung2025execution} used debugger-extracted traces as grounded supervision; \\\\citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces (recording only changed variables) perform comparably to full traces---supporting our finding that complete information is redundant.\\nCRUXEval \\\\citep{gu2024cruxeval} and CRUXEval-X \\\\citep{xu2025cruxevalx} established execution reasoning benchmarks.\\nNone of these works decompose which trace components contribute to \\\\emph{classification} accuracy or test cross-model generalization.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}\\nTRACED \\\\citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability and clone detection.\\nIn patch correctness, APPT \\\\citep{appt2024} achieved 79.7\\\\% with BERT+LSTM; LLM4PatchCorrect \\\\citep{zhou2024llm4patch} reached 84.4\\\\% zero-shot with execution traces; \\\\citet{yang2025apsg} proposed Graph-LoRA for parameter-efficient patch classification.\\nFuseFL \\\\citep{widyasari2024fusefl} fused fault localization with error messages; \\\\citet{foster2025mutation} deployed LLM equivalence detection at Meta with 0.95 precision.\\nThese approaches treat traces as monolithic inputs; none have isolated individual components or evaluated cross-model generalization.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}\\nTraceCoder \\\\citep{huang2026tracecoder} inserts diagnostic probes at selected locations; InspectCoder \\\\citep{wang2025inspectcoder} enables active debugger control for strategic breakpoints; \\\\citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited benefit and LLM-optimized formats outperform---supporting our finding that compression is essential.\\n\\\\citet{wu2026runtime} used selective variable snapshots in a multi-agent repair framework.\\nThese methods target repair (generation) rather than classification, and none analyze the information density trade-off.\\nOur gradient-dilution/information-overload decomposition provides the first quantitative framework for understanding \\\\emph{why} trace compression helps.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}
```

```text
The appendix looks good. Per-class table is detailed and complete. Training details are well-documented. Hardware is correctly listed as "RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell (94 GB VRAM)".

Now let me compile my full review of the round5-edits paper worker.

Key findings:

**Fixed issues:**
1. DebugBench conclusion bug — FIXED (now mentions "LiveCodeBench" as future work)
2. McNemar p-values — correctly attributed (B4: p<0.001, B5: p=0.041)


3. B5 format marker — properly explained as "preprocessing artifact"

**New/expanded content (good):**
- Cross-model section significantly expanded with tokenizer analysis, group analysis, Fisher exact test
- Discussion section well-written with honest "trivially solvable" acknowledgment
- 6 limitations listed (comprehensive)
- Related work expanded with more citations
- Appendix has detailed per-class breakdown

**Remaining issues:**
1. **ae_only "≥99.8%" claim** (severity: warning): Appears in both discussion and conclusion. Will be violated when 3-seed data is incorporated (seed123=99.08%). Need to soften to "≥99%" or similar.
2. **Table 2 reports seed42 only** (severity: medium): Should clarify this is single-seed results and mention that multi-seed evaluation is in progress.
3. **ae_only seed variance not yet in paper** (severity: info): Once the registry is updated, I'll need to incorporate this into the limitations section alongside the existing DeepSeek variance discussion.

The paper has made substantial progress and is ready to move forward with the review.
```

```json
  "text": "## 审查报告 — worker_paper_round5-edits\n\n**结论**: WARNING（整体质量大幅提升，有一个待修 claim）\n\n### 修复确认\n\n- [severity: info] **DebugBench 结论 bug 已修复**（此前报告 3 次）：conclusion.tex 不再将 DebugBench 列为 future work，改为 \"LiveCodeBench\"。§4.4 已报告 DebugBench 结果。✓\n- [severity: info] **McNemar p 值已正确归属**：B4 vs SFT p<0.001，B5 vs SFT p=0.041。✓\n- [severity: info] **B5 format marker 解释充分**：明确定义为 \"preprocessing artifact that leaks the verdict through surface formatting\"，区分于 genuine exception semantics。✓\n\n### 论文质量评估\n\n**强项**：\n- Cross-model 分析深入：tokenizer 分析、group analysis (intact 100% vs truncated 83.04%, Fisher p<0.001)、DeepSeek 双峰模式\n- Discussion 诚实承认 \"close to trivially solvable\"，将贡献定位在 (a) information density dilution 量化 和 (b) truncation confound\n- 6 条 limitations 全面且具体（含 DeepSeek seed variance σ=4.76pp）\n- Related work 覆盖广，positioning 清晰\n\n### 待修问题\n\n- [severity: warning] **\"≥99.8%\" claim 将被 ae_only seed123 违反**：Discussion 和 Conclusion 各出现一次 \"Tier 1 signals...individually achieve ≥99.8% accuracy\"。ae_only seed123=99.08% < 99.8%。当 ae_only 3-seed 数据录入后（mean=99.64%），此 claim 不成立。**建议改为 \"≥99%\" 或 \"≥99.0%\"**，或改为 \"achieve near-perfect accuracy (99.1--99.9%)\"。\n\n- [severity: info] **Table 2 使用 seed42 数据**：Caption 已注明 \"seed=42\"，当前可接受。但 ae_only 3-seed 均值 (99.64%) 与 seed42 (99.92%) 差 0.28pp，建议后续补充脚注或更新为 3-seed 均值。\n\n- [severity: info] **ae_only seed variance 尚未写入论文**：DeepSeek seed variance 已在 limitations 中讨论（σ=4.76pp），但 Qwen ae_only seed variance（σ=0.40pp, seed123=99.08%）未提及。虽然远小于 DeepSeek，但为完整性应补充。\n\n### 总结\n论文从 v4 到当前版本有实质性提升，主要 claim 均有数据支撑，叙事逻辑连贯。最大待修项是 \"≥99.8%\" 阈值——这是 ae_only 3-seed 数据录入后必须同步调整的硬约束。",
  "project": "trace-verdict-study"
}
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The worker registered new experiments for multi-family and multi-scale validation:

**CodeLlama-7B-Instruct** (new model family - LLaMA):
- codellama_exception_only_seed42
- codellama_vota_seed42
- codellama_no_trace_seed42

**Qwen-2.5-Coder-1.5B-Instruct** (smaller scale):
- qwen1.5b_vota_seed42
- qwen1.5b_full_trace_seed42

And probably more (total went from 53 to 60). 

These additions directly address reviewer feedback about generalization across models and scale sensitivity—adding CodeLlama as a third model family alongside Qwen and DeepSeek, plus testing at the 1.5B scale to see how the approach holds up with smaller models.

The timeline works out: with 7 new experiments taking roughly 2-2.5 hours each on GPU and 2 available GPUs, I can run them sequentially in pairs for about 4.5 wall-clock hours before the May 25 deadline. CodeLlama-7B-Instruct is from 2023 and not the absolute latest (Llama 3.1 and 4 exist now), but it's an established baseline in code LLM literature that's defensible for multi-family validation purposes.

The real point is that CodeLlama represents a different architecture family from Qwen and DeepSeek, which is what matters for diversity. There are newer LLaMA-based options like Llama 3.1 or StarCoder2, but since the paper already anchors on Qwen-2.5-Coder and DeepSeek-Coder as primary models, adding CodeLlama as a third family for breadth is reasonable even if it's not the most current option available.

I should flag this as a potential concern and let the Director decide whether the framing as "diverse model families" adequately addresses the currency issue. Now let me review the full list of new experiments to see what else needs checking.
```
